日本：旺盛なAI関連需要が製造業の景況感を押し上げ。利益計画は大幅上方修正
3
 大企業の業況判断は製造業が6四半期連続の改善。非製造業は悪化も一時的な悪天候が主因で変調の懸念は低い
― 業種別では、化学、石油・石炭製品、生産用機械等が改善。旺盛なAI関連需要や原油の供給不足懸念の後退等が背景

― 一方、非製造業は、長雨を受けて小売や対個人サービス、宿泊・飲食サービスが悪化
 2026年度の経常利益計画は、製造業中心に大幅上方修正。9月調査時点としては過去5年間で最高の増益計画
― とりわけ大企業・製造業では、輸出向け売上高の上方修正が顕著。上期の実績が大幅に上振れたことを反映したもの
― 世界的なAI関連需要の好調持続が見込まれるもと、下期も堅調な輸出が続けば、利益計画は一段の上方修正余地

日本：設備投資は高水準を維持。製造業は業績対比でなお上振れ余地
4
 2026年度の設備投資計画は前年度比＋9.3％と高い伸びを維持
― 製造業は6月調査からほぼ横ばいの同＋7.1％。非製造業は同＋11.8％と1.1％Ptの上方修正
 AI需要による好業績を踏まえれば製造業の投資計画にはやや慎重さがにじむ内容
― 一方、生産・営業用設備判断DIは2025年度後半以降、設備不足方向への動きが鮮明。高水準の内部資金も踏まえれ
ば、増産投資などを中心に今後の設備投資が上振れる余地
― なお、今後の利上げが企業の投資姿勢に及ぼす影響には留意
25年度後半以降、急速
な設備不足超を示唆

日本：企業の価格設定姿勢はなお強い。仕入価格再上昇にも警戒
5
 大企業・製造業の販売価格判断DIは前期並みの高水準を維持。企業は積極的な価格転嫁姿勢を継続
―6・7月の油価下落等を背景に素材業種の仕入価格判断DIが低下。対して販売価格判断DIは横ばい。コスト低下局面で
も販売価格を維持しており、企業利益の押し上げ要因に
 企業の中長期的な物価見通しも2％台半ばと、日銀の物価目標を上回る水準で高止まり
― 足元の原油価格再上昇やタンカー運賃高騰を受け、仕入価格が再び上昇する可能性には留意。企業の価格設定姿勢
が一段と強まるリスクも
販売価格判断DI・仕入価格判断DI（大企業・製造業） 物価全般の見通し（全規模・全産業）

日本：利上げでも企業金融は安定。企業部門の金利耐性を確認
6
 資金繰り判断は総じて底堅い。これまでの利上げの影響は限定的
― 利上げの影響が懸念された中小企業の資金繰り判断は横ばい圏の推移。価格転嫁の進展などを受けた事業環境の改
善が資金繰りを支えている構図
― 金利上昇局面でも銀行貸出は増加基調を維持しており、企業の資金需要も底堅く推移
 企業収益や資金繰りが金利上昇への耐性を示す中、金融環境はなお緩和的
― 市場のターミナルレート予想は2.5％程度まで上昇し、日銀の中立金利推計レンジ上限に接近

（%）
欧州：G7は原油・ディーゼルの協調放出で合意。米国の輸出制限リスクは後退
7
 G7はIEAを通じ4カ月で1億バレルの石油備蓄放出に合意。初期20日間はディーゼルを前倒し放出
― 背景にディーゼル価格高騰。中間選挙を控えたトランプ大統領は、禁輸も辞さない姿勢を強調（米国は世界需要の4.9％
を供給）。米国からの輸出が欧州の需要を支える一方、米国よりも潤沢な在庫を保有する欧州に圧力をかけた格好
― 欧州は石油製品消費に占めるディーゼル比率が高く、価格高騰を回避する思惑も協調放出の合意を後押し
― 合意ではG7域内でのエネルギー輸出制限回避も確認され、米国によるEU向け禁輸リスクはひとまず後退
石油製品の製品別消費割合の国際比較
（出所）Energy Institute より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
EUの石油製品消費はディーゼル・軽油が中心

### Page 9

欧州：ディーゼル需給逼迫は残存。欧州のインフレ上振れ要因に
8
 ロシアのディーゼル輸出は足元で急減。中東からの輸出減、中国の輸出停止も重なり、世界需要の約6％が失われた格好
―G7の1億バレル放出で当面の供給不安は緩和するものの、構造的な供給不足の解消には至らず
―IEAも、中東からの原油輸出は回復する一方、石油製品の供給制約はなお深刻と指摘。ディーゼル需給の逼迫が続け
ば、輸送・物流コストを通じたインフレ圧力が残存
 欧州では9月HICPが再加速。ディーゼル高が輸送・物流コストを通じて追加的なインフレ上振れ要因となるリスク
―9月消費者物価（HICP）は前年比＋3.8％（前月：同＋3.2％）と伸び加速。エネルギー価格の上昇が主因
今般の備蓄放出量と停止中のディーゼル輸出
（注） 世界需要およびロシア・中国の輸出量は2025年実績。中東は2026年8月実績を参
考に、輸出量を前年の4分の1としてシェアを概算
（出所）EIA、IEA、OPEC、各種報道より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

注目点②G7備蓄放出でもくすぶるディーゼル供給不安

米国：雇用は減速も、AI関連需要が下支え
 9月の非農業部門雇用者数は前月差＋2.9万人と市場予想（同＋9.0万人）を下回る結果。7月・8月も下方修正
― 民間部門が前月差＋4.6万人（8月同+8.9万人）と増勢が鈍化したほか、政府部門が同▲1.7万人と再びマイナス転化
― 民間部門では、旺盛なAI投資需要などを背景に、製造や建設、建設・エンジニアリングなどが増加基調を維持したもの
の、情報、金融、専門サービスなどの高賃金セクターが減少
― 時間当たり賃金の伸びは前月比＋0.1％（8月同＋0.3％）と鈍化。こちらも市場予想（同＋0.3％）を下回る結果
― 総じて冴えない結果ながら、3カ月平均では＋5万人程度を維持しており、雇用は減速しつつも失速には至らず

9
（出所） 米労働省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

米国：対人型業種の雇用に底堅さ。労働参加率上昇で失業率は小幅上昇
 対人接触度の高い業種では雇用に底堅さ、AIによる代替が進みにくい可能性
― ヘルスケアや建設、飲食サービスなど、対人接触機会の多い業種では雇用が増加する一方、経理・会計サービス、ITシ
ステム開発などでは低調
 失業率は4.2％と小幅悪化も引き続き低水準を維持
― 主に16-24歳の若年層およびプライムエイジ層の労働参加率の上昇が押し上げに寄与した模様

中国：景況感は急速に改善。建設業が持ち直し。政府は住宅ローンの利子補給で市場テコ入れ
 9月の総合PMIは50.7と、前月（49.5）から大幅に上昇。景況感が急速に改善。建設業が大きく持ち直し
― 製造業PMIが50.1（前月：49.8）、非製造業PMIが50.2（前月：49.0）とともに節目回復。製造業の生産（51.7）が堅調
― 建設業PMIは50.3と、前月（46.9）から急上昇。異常気象の影響はく落と財政予算執行の本格化があるとみられる
 中国政府が9/28、新規住宅ローン金利の年率1％分を補助する政策を発表。低迷が続く不動産市場をテコ入れ
― 対象物件の購入金額を150万元（3,500万円）以下に限定し、低中所得者層の住宅ローン負担を軽減する
― これにより、余剰在庫に苦しむ地方都市での実需を掘り起こし、市況のさらなる悪化に歯止めをかけたい考え

### Page 13

 中国のASEAN向け輸出品は、ASEANの製造品と競合。競合分野は一般機械や玩具、食品など幅広に
― 一般機械等に加え、ベトナムは電子機器や紙・木・繊維等の軽工業品、インドネシアは輸送機械でも競合度が高い
 中国製品流入が強まる中、ASEANはアンチダンピング、セーフガード措置に加え、越境ECへの規制強化などで対応
― 素材・中間財についてはアンチダンピング措置で対応。最終財では、低価格輸入品への課税や価格規制で応じる方向
― 中国企業等による規制回避を懸念し、ASEAN側が対象品目拡張や反迂回措置を強化する動きもあるが、こうした措置
の実効性は不透明
アジア：中国製品との競争激化でASEANの防衛策が拡大

 2026年以降に相違が目立つ台湾と韓国におけるAI需要の恩恵

日本：コアCPIは政策補助効果のはく落を主因に加速。その背後で徐々に高まるインフレ圧力
14
 9月の都区部コアCPIは前年比＋2.7％（前月：同＋1.8％）と大幅加速。政策要因のはく落に加え、価格転嫁の影響も拡大
― 東京都で実施された水道基本料金無償化・保育所保育料無償化の影響がはく落し、全体の伸びを＋0.56％Pt押し上げ

か？

市場焦點
美國
科技股
• 美國企業獲利維持強勁 ，背後主要受經濟展望穩健及 AI資本支出熱潮支撐 。從獲利來看 ，市
場共識預期標普500企業EPS成長率2026年達28%，2027年及2028年則分別為20%及16%，
雖呈現逐步降溫 ，但仍維持雙位數增長 。標普500預期本益比已由約 23倍降至19倍，且並非
主要來自股價修正 ，而是企業獲利預期上調所致 ，在企業獲利維持成長 、AI投資持續挹注及
估值已較高點收斂的情況下，標普500仍具中長期配置價值。
美債
外匯對沖視角
• 在全球公債遭遇拋售潮與通膨陰影下 ，美債因利率期間結構與匯率優勢 ，今年以美元計價總
回報優於多數G7主權債，儘管海外私人機構投資人正在等待長端利率落底訊號以重返市場 ，
對美元資金或以美元計價為主的投資人而言 ，當前美債名目殖利率仍處於數十年高位 ，具備

相當良好的長期鎖利價值，但價格波動風險考量下，建議債券佈局仍以中短天期為主。
台股
• 8月景氣對策燈號續亮紅燈，綜合判斷分數41分，較前月持平，對應的燈號仍是代表景氣熱絡
的紅燈，連續第9個月亮出紅燈，追平史上最長「連紅」紀錄，顯示國內經濟維持成長。
• 根據歷史統計第四季台股上漲的機率接近八成 ，除了AI投資趨勢未減 ，年底集團作帳行情以
及美國11月期中選後不確定性下降有望支撐台股多頭情緒 。群益投顧樂觀看待台股第四季表
現，有望上看53,000點。此外，彭博綜合統計台灣加權指數12個月目標價達59,688點。

就業增幅明顯放緩，降低Fed短期再升息急迫性
• 非農明顯降溫，勞動市場尚未出現全面惡化：9月非農僅增2.9萬人，遠低於預期，且7、8月合計下修6萬人；失業率升
至4.2%，但勞參率同步升至 61.8%，家戶調查就業增加 40.6萬人，顯示失業率上升部分來自勞動供給增加 ，其中營建、
製造業仍分別增加1.1萬及0.9萬人，平均工時維持34.4小時，數據呈現招聘動能明顯放慢，但尚未出現全面性就業惡化。
• 薪資增速放緩，Fed短期政策壓力下降：9月平均時薪月增僅0.1%、年增3.0%，均低於前值；同時8月PCE年增3.4%、
核心PCE年增3.0%，顯示薪資通膨壓力有所緩和 ，但整體物價仍高於Fed 2%目標，在9月Fed升息1碼後，非農報告降
低Fed在10月連續升息的必要性，市場焦點轉向年底通膨與AI投資驅動的產業動能。

• 投資建議：短期關注美國中短天期投資級債券 ，就業降溫與薪資放緩有助降低短端利率上行壓力 ，但通膨仍高於目標 ，
因此不宜過度 預期長天期利率快速回落；股市則可續關注 AI基礎建設與資本支出相關產業 ，因營建及製造業就業仍具
韌性，顯示企業投資動能尚未明顯轉弱。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics， 2026.10.3

消費動能回升、通膨低於預期，內需韌性與物價壓力並存
• PCE低於預期、通膨降溫仍待確認：美國8月PCE物價指數月增0.3%、年增3.4%，核心PCE月增0.2%、年增3.0%，均低於
市場預期；其中核心PCE較修訂後7月的3.3%下降，顯示物價壓力有所緩解，但仍高於Fed 2%目標，此次年度更新回溯修
訂2021年以來資料，亦使歷史基期重新調整，後續仍須觀察通膨下降是否能延續。
• 消費回升、所得增幅偏緩：8月個人所得月增0.2%，個人消費支出（PCE）月增0.9%、實質消費成長0.6%，顯示內需仍具
韌性；惟消費增幅明顯高於所得 ，個人儲蓄率由7月4.6%降至4.1%，為2022年11月以來低點，反映家庭可支應消費的財
務緩衝持續縮小，後續消費動能仍須觀察。
• 後市觀察：PCE低於預期後，市場對10月再升息的預期明顯降溫，但長端美債殖利率仍受通膨、財政供給與期限溢價牽動

而維持高檔；建議可聚焦獲利成長與現金流穩健的優質大型成長股與短天期美債以掌握收益 ，同時留意儲蓄率下滑與 9月
消費者信心低迷可能抑制後續消費動能，動態調整部位並嚴控風險。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis， 2026.10.1

能源與AI推升長端利率，融資成本壓力延續
• 長端殖利率上行 、期限溢價成重要推力 ：近期
美債呈現短端與長端走勢分化 ，雖市場對 10月
Fed再升息預期因 8月PCE低於預期而明顯降溫 ，
10年期及30年期的美債殖利率仍偏高位 ，反映
通膨風險 、財政融資需求與期限溢價上升 ，長
天期無風險利率維持高檔 ，並推升房貸及企業
融資成本。
• 能源與AI投資推升通膨及資金需求 ：油價維持
高檔增加通膨黏著風險 ，AI基礎建設投資則帶
動企業資本支出與融資需求 ，兩者共同提高長
期資金需求；在財政赤字與美債供給仍大的環
境下，長端殖利率可能維持較高水準 ，進而提
高股票估值折現率及高槓桿企業的融資壓力。
• 後市觀察： 短期持續觀察油價 、10年及30年期
美債殖利率與期限溢價變化；債券配置仍建議

降低長天期債券部位以降低利率風險 ，優先布
局短天期高品質債券獲取穩健息收 ，股票則聚
焦具穩健現金流 、低財務槓桿與定價能力的企
業，以降低高利率環境下的估值與融資風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，Federal Reserve Bank of New Tork ， 2026.10.1

美光最新一季財報與財測 2026會計年度Q4實際 市場預期 2027會計年度Q1財測
營收 542.3億美元 約504.5-510.7億美元 615億美元 ±15億美元

自由現金流 590億美元 - -
AI需求大增，美光財測超過市場預期
• AI記憶體需求強勁，美光財測顯示景氣延續：美光科技9/30盤後公布財報，2026財年的第四季營收達542.3億美元，年增

379%，調整後EPS 33.42美元，均優於市場預期；公司並預估2027會計年度第一季營收615億美元、調整後EPS 38.15美元，
分別高於市場預期的567.7億美元及36.02美元。強勁財測反映AI資料中心持續推升HBM、伺服器DRAM及高速SSD需求。
隨Amazon、Alphabet等大型科技企業持續擴大AI基礎建設投資，記憶體需求仍具成長動能。
• 長約與產品升級，支撐中長期業績：美光目前已簽署36份多年期供應協議，涵蓋約三分之一營收並延伸至2030年，有助降
低記憶體產業週期波動；同時持續布局HBM4、HBM4E及高容量DDR5等產品。公司2026會計年度自由現金流達590億美元，
並預期2027、2028會計年度供需將進一步緊俏。整體而言，AI需求已由單一HBM擴展至伺服器記憶體與儲存產品，美光財
測亦顯示景氣延續性仍強，後續市場焦點將轉向擴產速度、資本支出及AI需求成長能否維持。
資料來源：美光，彭博，鉅亨網，2026.10.1

• AI驅動獲利成長，企業基本面仍具支撐：美國企業獲利維持強勁，背後主要受經濟展望穩健及AI資本支出熱潮支撐。從獲利
來看，市場共識預期標普500企業EPS成長率2026年達28%，2027年及2028年則分別為20%及16%，雖呈現逐步降溫，但
仍維持雙位數增長。其中，AI基礎建設與大型雲端業者仍是重要驅動來源，2026年對EPS成長的貢獻合計約19%，2027、
2028年仍分別貢獻14%及11%。
• 獲利上修降低估值壓力：標普500預期本益比已由約23倍降至19倍，且並非主要來自股價修正，而是企業獲利預期上調所致。
雖然未來EPS成長率若由高檔回落，市場仍可能面臨評價修正壓力，但在企業獲利維持成長、AI投資持續挹注及估值已較高
點收斂的情況下，標普500基本面仍具支撐。因此，短期應留意獲利增速放緩與市場持倉偏高所帶來的波動，但從獲利成長

與估值兩大面向觀察，標普500仍具中長期配置價值。

中國製造業PMI(%) 企業利潤前瞻指數反彈上行(%)
BCI:企業利潤前瞻指數 BCI:企業銷售前瞻指數
2026年製造業PMI 2024年 2025年 2023年
生產回升與價格上漲同步推動中國PMI改善
• 生產回升與價格上漲同步推動 中國PMI改善： 9月製造業PMI升至50.1%，較前月回升0.3個百分點，重返榮枯線以上，顯示
製造業景氣有所改善 ，主要受生產活動回暖及價格上漲帶動 ，中小企業景氣同步改善 ，但仍低於榮枯線，大型企業表現相對
較佳，高技術製造、裝備製造維持擴張 ，消費品PMI升至50.7%，重回景氣區間 ，另一方面，原材料及能源價格上漲推升企
業成本，上下游獲利差距持續擴大 。 非製造業PMI則由49.0%升至50.2%，服務業受節慶效應帶動回溫 ，建築業則受財政資

金投入，及基建建設推進而改善，不過需求復甦仍偏溫和，企業成本壓力尚未明顯減輕。
• 結論：第四季隨著基建投資、財政支出及房市支持政策逐步落地，經濟可望獲得支撐，但10月受長假因素影響，PMI可能短
期承壓。
資料來源： 萬聯證券，2026.9.30資料來源： 萬聯證券，2026.9.30

• 8月景氣對策燈號續亮紅燈： 綜合判斷分數41分，較前月持平，對應的燈號仍是代表景氣熱絡的紅燈 ，連續第9個月亮出紅

設，民間投資有望維持動能。此外，受惠財富效應、企業調薪及就業市場穩定，民間消費有望持續成長。
• 法人看好台股年底前站上5萬點：根據歷史統計第四季台股上漲的機率接近八成 ，除了AI投資趨勢未減，年底集團作帳行情
以及美國11月期中選後不確定性下降有望支撐台股多頭情緒 。群益投顧樂觀看待台股第四季表現 ，有望上看53,000點。此
外，彭博綜合統計台灣加權指數12個月目標價達59,688點。

的得天獨厚。年底前仍須留意中東衝突進展，若能源通膨壓力持續，聯準會升息預期恐為股市帶來波動，但考慮AI科技持續
發酵，建議投資人續抱台股，並以定期不定額的策略紀律加碼，目標追求中長期完整的AI趨勢回報。

外匯對沖視角—美債對沖後殖利率屬全球最低之一
• 美國國債殖利率真的高嗎？在當前全球總體經濟環境下，美國國債殖利率雖維持在數十年歷史高點附近，但若單憑名目殖利
率來判斷美債的吸引力，容易忽視匯率對沖與各國財政風險帶來的溢價差異。
• 美元視角下的殖利率比較： 若將其他已開發市場的主權債務 (如德債、日債、英債)完全對沖回美元，美債在殖利率曲線上多
數期限的實際殖利率，其實與德債並列全球最低水準。相反地，日本國債與英國國債經匯率對沖回美元後的收益率反而更高。
• 跨貨幣資產交換 (ASW)的真實回報： 以10年期主權債的對沖跨貨幣資產交換計算：美國 10年期國債有效殖利率約 4.85%，
日本10年期國債(對沖至美元)有效殖利率約5.06%，德國10年期國債(對沖至美元)有效殖利率約4.51%，這表明美國政府發

債的真實對沖融資成本其實已相對精實，且符合國際市場對各國財政基本面的合理定價。
對沖至美元後的10年期主權債殖利率

財政基本面與信用風險—殖利率溢價反映國家債務比重
• 從歐元計價或信用風險的角度來比較 ，美債的定價邏輯非常符合總體經濟基本面： 儘管債務以歐元計價時 ，10年期美債殖
利率比同期限德國國債高出約30個基點，這反映出德國的債務佔GDP比重僅約65%，而美國則高達127%，因此美債承擔了
適度的財政溢價。儘管義大利的債務佔GDP比重高達140%且信用前景較遜色，但在統一貨幣基準下，義大利國債殖利率仍
較美國國債高出60個基點，這顯示市場並未過度壓低美債信用，美債依然享有優質避險資產的溢價優勢。
• 海外私人投資者的資金流向：近一年海外私人機構在美債加碼速度放緩，乃通膨環境下全球其他資產選擇增加，及過去升息
波段導致帳面按市值計價虧損 。然而歐洲投資人(持有一半以上的海外美債)目前仍持有龐大部位，並在等待適當的加碼時機。

• 小結與投資建議：在經過外匯對沖後，美債收益率在發達國家主權債中處於相對低位，發行成本合理且具競爭力。美債殖利
率高於德債低於義債 ，精確地反映美國政府債務負擔率 (127%)與總體經濟穩健度 。在全球公債遭遇拋售潮與通膨陰影下 ，
美債因利率期間結構與匯率優勢 ，今年以美元計價總回報優於多數 G7主權債，儘管海外私人機構投資人正在等待長端利率
落底訊號以重返市場，對美元資金或以美元計價為主的投資人而言，當前美債名目殖利率仍處於數十年高位，具備相當良好
的長期鎖利價值，但價格波動風險考量下，建議債券佈局仍以中短天期為主。

亞洲晶⽚債券跑輸指數，評級保持穩健
• 亞洲晶⽚債券跑輸指數，評級保持穩健：儘管AI熱潮推動收⼊創下紀錄 ，但SK海力⼠和台積電的美元債 ，今年以來的表現仍
略遜於彭博亞洲美元信用債總回報指數 ，主因為大規模資本支出擴張 ，以及潛在的投⼊資本回報率下降令 其利差承壓，美國
雲服務巨頭創紀錄的 AI支出計畫加劇了壓力 ，儘管如此 SK 海力⼠和台積電仍保持較強的高評級信用實力 ，台積電計畫投
⼊600-640億美元資本支出 ，SK海力⼠的資本支出則處於 40兆韓圜區間。兩家公司穩健的資產負債表 、合計共1,260億美元
的淨現金、強勁的經營性現金流，以及在先進製程，和HBM晶⽚領域的市場領先地位，為上述支出提供支撐。
• 投資建議：現階段台積電與SK海力⼠美元債最大的問題不是信用風險 ，而是估值與利差壓力 ，短期可能因AI資本支出過大，

及市場情緒波動而表現落後指數 ，但以兩家公司強大的現金流 、淨現金部位及產業領導地位來看 ，信用品質仍屬亞洲投資級
債中的優質標的，建議利差擴大時分批布局 。

顧 • 企業獲利成長將支撐股價上行：標普500指數的長期走勢與企業每股盈餘（EPS）高度連動。高盛預估，標普500企業EPS將
於2026年達375美元，並在2027年進一步升至415美元，對應年增率約36%與11%。在利率維持高檔、金融條件偏緊的環境
下，企業獲利仍持續處於成長軌道，將為股市提供穩健的中期上行動能。
• 本益比回落提供潛在布局契機：根據FactSet數據，標普500企業第三季整體獲利預計成長30.6%。其中，資訊科技類股獲利
成長預估高達66.5%，領先能源以外的其他產業；同時，該類股預估本益比已由今年高點回落，在獲利上修與估值修正同步
發生的背景下，浮現具吸引力的中期布局機會。
• 小結：短期市場仍可能受地緣政治與利率預期擾動，但中期趨勢仍由企業獲利擴張所主導。建議投資人採取分批布局科技股

策略，聚焦具備AI受益結構、資本支出能見度高及現金流穩健的龍頭企業。

前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊 ，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣 (DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險 。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。

### Page 1

Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co.

3Q26 earnings preview: steeper 2nm ramp 
lifting sales upside at resilient margins

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 3,260 TWD | Price: 2,550 TWD

Solid HPC and flagship mobile pipeline solidifies 2H26 
TSMC’s 3Q26 sales were above street +18% QoQ in NT$ and reached US$46.7bn (vs. its 
guide of US$44.6-45.8bn) which we believe should be driven by the builds for Apple’s 
mobile SoC and AMD’s server CPU on 2nm along with multiple GPU and ASIC product 
refreshes migrating to 3nm. The mix upgrades could lift 4Q26 sales by +10-15% QoQ, 
putting it on track to the guidance for 40%+ YoY in ’26. While utilization and pricing are 
favorable, GMs could hold steady in 3Q/4Q26 (BofAe 67.0%/67.8%) rather than expand 
(recent street 67-68%/68-69%) factoring dilution from 2nm and overseas fab ramps. 
Tight supply enables greater value capture and investment 
On solid AI demand outlook, TSMC’s capex should be on track to its lifted guidance of 
US$60-64bn in ’26 (BofAe US$63bn). The investment could see another surge in ’27 
(BofAe US$83bn) for further N3, N2, A16 and CoWoS-L expansion and new technologies 
ramp (e.g. CoPoS and CPO). The tool makers’ modest price hike could require TSMC to 
spend 10% more capex though should be reflected in its value when the capacity starts 
production in ’28 on top of a 5-10% leading edge ASP lift in 1Q27 on supply tightness. 
Checkpoints: AI demand, capex & competition/partnership 
1) TSMC’s view on cloud AI investment amid rate hike + challenging FCF, 2) comment on 
the market and its growth and capex commitment, 3) update on the competition and 
collaboration (see Exhibit 29 for our analysis on Terafab/SpaceX) and US investment.  
AI-led earnings outlook firmer; valuation should catch up 
We lift our 26/27/28 EPS by 2-9% to NT$112/NT$161/NT$203 and keep our Buy rating 
on TSMC and raise PO to NT$3,260/US$620 on 20x ‘27E P/E. Its current valuation is 
undemanding at 16x ‘27E P/E (vs. its long term 11-21x) backed by its advanced 
technology leadership.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
TSMC was founded in 1987 and is listed in Taiwan 
(2330TT) and the US (TSM/NYS).TSMC is the largest and 
global leader in integrated circuit (IC) manufacturing. As a 
build-to-order foundry, it provides a wide range of value-
add activities: IC manufacturing, mask-making, IC design 
services, turnkey solutions, and process development. We 
attribute its success to its proven, winning business model, 
unparalleled scale advantage, optimized execution, and 
technology scope and depth. 5 shares = 1 ADR.

Investment Rationale 
The ongoing semi content growth in mobile, rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI), and proliferation of Internet of Things (IoT) 
should result in sustainable upside in aggregate computing 
power globally. TSMC, as the leading contract manufacturer 
of semiconductor chips, is in a good position to capitalize. 
We are structurally positive on TSMC and expect it to 
sustain over 15% growth with rising FCF from 2026 
onwards.

### Page 4

3Q26 sales topped guidance; GMs tracking
well but recent street could be too bullish
We profile our expectation for TSMC’s 3Q26 earnings on October 15th. 3Q26 sales were
reported +18% QoQ in NT$, above BofAe/street expectations of +14%/+13% QoQ.
Based on the average FX of NT$31.99/US$ (vs. its NT$32US$ guide), its sales in US$
term would be US$46.7bn, also above its guidance range of US$44.6-45.8bn. we believe
the strength is led by strong ramp on 2nm potentially growing to 11% of its sales in

3Q26 (vs. 3% in 2Q26) for Apple’s builds for its product launches and surging demand
for AMD’s Venice server CPU. These growth drivers on the node are supplemented by
the incremental inventory stocking from Mediatek and Qualcomm for their flagship
smartphone SoCs albeit at a low base and below seasonal due to weak Android demand.
On top of the 2nm scale up, we expect the utilization for 5nm and 3nm to stay above
100% backed by the continued HPC/AI build strength especially with multiple product

upgrades including 1) accelerators (GPUs and ASICs): strong demand for Nvidia’s Rubin
GPU, AMD’s MI400, AWS’s Trainium3 ramp and initial ramp for Google’s TPUv8t/i more
than offsetting a mild wind down in Google’s Ironwood TPU, 2) CPUs: solid demand for
general purpose servers and AI data centers as noted in our deep dive report quantifying
CPU demand through ’30 across x86 (AMD’s Turin CPUs) and ARM based CPUs (e.g.
Nvidia’s Vera CPU, Google’s Axion CPU, AWS Graviton CPU along with incremental

contribution from Microsoft Cobalt CPU) and 3) networking switch: more and faster
data transmission in the complex AI data centers requires more advanced  networking
switch solutions from Nvidia/Mellanox, Broadcom and Marvell.
The company’s better than expected sales in 3Q26 backed by healthy shipment and
pricing trend on the advanced nodes should be supportive to its GMs. However, in
contrast to the recent aggressive street expectations of 67-68% GMs in 3Q26, we only

model it at the high-end of its guidance of 65-67%, balancing more favorable utilization
and gradual 3nm depreciation roll off partially offset by a 3-4% GMs dilution from 2nm
ramp which we believe is driver for the incremental sales during the quarter.

### Page 5

HPC/Apple lifting 4Q26 sales outlook;
resilient GM expectation but below street
For 4Q26, based on its slightly above +40% YoY full year sales guide, TSMC’s sales could
grow 10-15% QoQ (BofAe +11%), better than its 10-year seasonality of 7% QoQ even
off a high base from 1Q-3Q26. The blended ASPs strength (BofAe +11% QoQ) should be
the key driver for 4Q26 sales as shipment mix continues to shift toward advanced nodes,
with revenue from 5nm and below processes representing 75% of the company’s sales

(vs. 66%/71% in 2Q/3Q26). Specifically, 2nm with 15%+ higher wafer pricing than 3nm
could further grow its contribution to 18% of TSMC’s sales during the quarter. On the
other hand, we model a stable wafer shipment (BofAe +9%/-5% QoQ for
advanced/mature nodes), balancing HPC demand strength and Apple’s seasonal chipset
builds for its new product launches and a modest drop on the specialty process output
and effective capacity loss due to equipment movement for the node conversion.

While the blended pricing is even more favorable and utilization on the advanced and
mature nodes in general stay healthy, we model a relatively stable GMs only up to
~67.8% levels in 4Q26 (vs. recent street and investors’ expectations at 68-69%). The key
factors we consider as mildly negative offset would be 1) higher depreciation: +25-30%
HoH in 2H26 due to more overseas and 2nm capacity ramp and 2) 2nm still going
through yield learning curve and could see more meaningful impact in 4Q26 (vs. its 3-4%

GMs dilution guide in 2H26) as the sales contribution grows.

### Page 6

2026-27 sales and margins outlook 
solidified by healthy AI demand 
Factoring in stronger CPU and AI GPU/ASIC demand outlook as noted in our Semicon 
Taiwan takeaways report where we updated our industry models, we are lifting our US$ 
sales expectation for TSMC in ‘26/’27 to +42%/+40% YoY respectively (vs. BofA prior 
+41%/+40% YoY). More importantly, with utilization staying tighter for longer along with 
inflationary cost pass through (e.g. equipment and consumables), TSMC should raise its 
advanced node and packaging pricing by 5-10% in the beginning of 2027 to reflect its 
value. This along with a mild 3-5% price hike on the mature nodes where the utilization 
is back to 90-95% as it optimizes the product mix following the strategic 6”/8” capacity 
retirement should lift its GMs to 67%/69% in 2026/27 (vs. BofA prior 67%/67%).

Capex on track for lifted guide for 2026 
and will grow 20%+ CAGR from ’26-28 
We lifted our front-end foundry industry capex growth expectation to 19%/26% YoY for 
‘26/27 to US$86bn/US$108bn, supporting a CAGR of 16% from ’26-28. This is mostly 
led by our higher expectation on TSMC’s capex on stronger wafer demand on N3 and N2 
from GPUs, ASICs, CPUs, networking switches and HBM base dies and initial ramp of 
A16, along with incremental spending reacceleration from tier 2 foundries. 
Separately, we also raised the back-end packaging and testing industry spending 
estimates from 66%/36% YoY to 82%/21% YoY for ‘26/27 to US$27bn/US$33bn, 
supporting a CAGR of 15% from ’26-28. The upward revision is driven by our higher 
expectation of TSMC’s more aggressive capacity addition on CoWoS-R/L to 
accommodate more demand from its customers amid growing level of chipset 
complexity and reticle size and higher level of capacity readiness of SoIC for higher 3D 
packaging adoption for logic and memory dies integration and CPO. The more 
aggressive spending from ASE is set to grow by another 30-50% YoY in 2027 off a high 
base in 2026 on its own FoCoS capacity ramp and TSMC’s CoWoS licensing.

Exhibit 12: Back-end sales, capex, and capital intensity

BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

Specifically on TSMC, based on our bottom up capacity analysis across front-end 
manufacturing and back-end packaging, TSMC’s capex spending is tracking to US$63bn 
for 2026 (vs. its latest guidance of US$60-64bn) mostly skewed toward advanced front-
end manufacturing including 55-60k WPM 3nm capacity addition, 60k WPM 2nm 
capacity ramp, initial 3-5k WPM A16 capacity installation. The back-end packaging 
investment should be largely driven by its CoWoS capacity growth while the spending on 
the equipment pull-in for CPO and CoPoS remains limited as most of the equipment are 
still under sampling process for qualification and meaningful shipment will be in 2027-
28. 
In addition to the earlier tool pull-in schedule due to tighter equipment supply with 
memory makers expanding, the fast-growing capex should reflect the rising cost per 1k 
WPM on the advanced nodes vs. mature nodes, with the equipment cost per 1k WPM 
advanced wafer capacity expansion has grown from US$156mn in 2023 to US$241mn in 
2025. Even factoring in more common use of equipment on the advanced nodes, the 
capex/1k WPM advanced capacity could further rise by 20% to US$289mn in 2027 with 
the technology architecture upgrade. 
On top of the investment in the core front-end manufacturing, we expect the company 
to slightly outspend in the back-end packaging and estimate it is at the upper half of its 
guidance for 10-20% total capex, mainly for more aggressive capacity expansion to 
meet its customers’ demand amid growing competition from Intel’s EMIB-T solution and 
technology innovation. On the other hand, the maintenance capex could be reduced for 
specialty technologies as the company gradually retires the equipment.

Capacity expansion accelerates across 
advanced front-end and back-end 
As TSMC is in the process to retire a portion of its equipment on 6”, 8” and mature 12” 
processes while still adding the capacity on the advanced nodes, we estimate the 
company’s total front-end manufacturing capacity will drop by 2% YoY in 2026. 
Exhibit 17: TSMC's year-end capacity by node for 20nm and below 
TSMC will expand aggressively on 3nm and 2nm through 2027

Source: Company Data, BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

Exhibit 18: TSMC's advanced node capacity as % of its installed base 
TSMC's advanced nodes will reach 40% of its total capacity

Source: Company Data, BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

The trend could be more stable into 2027-28 as TSMC strives to optimize its capacity 
mix and across node conversion to accommodate its customers’ demand especially on 
the leading-edge technologies, with its advanced capacity set to grow by 10% CAGR 
from 2025-28 balanced by the mature capacity reduction at -8% CAGR. 
Based on our estimate, TSMC’s capacity for 7nm and below nodes will grow from 320k 
WPM per month in 2023 to ~700k WPM by 2028 at a 16% CAGR, through expansion and 
efficient conversion from higher equipment commonality, cross technology planning and 
AI productivity gains (e.g. tool idle time and chamber cleaning schedule optimization). 
This will also support the mix of 7nm and below capacity climb toward 40-45% of its 
total capacity (vs. 24% in 2023). By region, we expect the capacity expansion through 
2027 to enable its overseas fabs to account for 17% of TSMC’s total capacity by 2028 
(vs. 7% in 2020) and similarly for the advanced node capacity (vs. ~0% in 2023), mainly 
in Arizona and Kumamoto. 
Specifically, we believe the company is on track to its target on 25% CAGR capacity 
expansion for its 5nm and 3nm from ’22-27. We model its 3nm capacity will reach 
190k/230k WPM by 4Q26/27. On N2 family, TSMC plans to grow capacity by 70% CAGR 
from ’26-28, with 1st year ramp delivering 45% more capacity vs. 3nm as it rolls out 5 
fabs at once.

On the back-end packaging, with surging server CPU demand on top of the already 
strong order backlog for GPUs and ASICs, we estimate the industry’s CoWoS supply will 
need to grow by 52% CAGR from 2026-28. TSMC will still lead the capacity expansion

in 4Q26) and 25-30k WPM (vs. 15-20k by 4Q26) in 4Q27 respectively. Specifically, while 
TSMC should still lead the supply for complex GPU and ASIC packaging, ASE grows as a 
major 2nd source with its capacity share growing from 18% of the industry in ’26 to 29% 
in ’28 and proven track record as the major CoWoS full process service provider for 
AMD’s Venice server CPU.

### Page 14

Solid technology roadmap and execution 
easing competition concern 
TSMC is pursuing a dual track strategy for its front-end manufacturing, advancing the 
solution for both its consumer and HPC customers. The nodes targeted for consumer 
chipsets requiring cost efficiency and IP reuse will be introduced on an annual basis to 
match its customers’ yearly product launch schedule. For the HPC applications, the 
company targets to roll out a new node with biennial cadence, supporting major leaps in 
performance, transistor density, and power density. 
Exhibit 27: Front-end manufacturing roadmap comparison 
TSMC's advanced node technology roadmap advancing faster vs. Intel and Samsung and has the widest offerings targeted for different applications

Source: BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH 
This is evidenced in its N2 family and below nodes, with N2U ramping in ’28 for mobile 
applications and A13 node in ’29 which will be backward compatible to A14. It will also 
have A12 node in ’29 integrated SPR to address power integrity and current delivery 
constraint suitable for AI/HPC systems. The strategy along with the continued expansion 
of the derivative offerings for main nodes should position TSMC well to defend its 
market share with its competitors claiming their progress on the advanced 
manufacturing. 
Specifically, Intel’s 18A has been in mass production since 4Q25 for its Panther Lake 
and Clearwater Forest chipsets though yield will not be fully fine tuned until 1Q27 at 25-
30k WPM capacity scale in its Oregon and Arizona fabs. The follow on derivative 
technology, 18A-P, has recently started risk production featuring 9% performance gains 
though may not enter into mass production until 2027 at the earliest. 
Longer term, Intel’s next major upgrade will be on 14A which is guided to start risk 
production in 2028 for commercialization in 2029. It is important to note A14 will be the 
first major node Intel provides with its external foundry customers and 0.9 PDK is 
targeted to be shipped in 4Q26. 
For Samsung, at its recent SAFE Forum in 2Q26, the company reaffirmed four 
generations of 2nm roadmap, including SF2 which has been in production since 2025, 
SF2P in 2026, SF2P+ in 2027-28 and SF2X (no firm date). Notably, the previously 
announced derivative technologies, including SF2Z featuring backside power for HPC 
applications and SF2A for automotive applications, were not included in its latest 
roadmap which should be folded as part of its SF2P+ offerings. With a modest delay for 
its 2nm progress, the company also pushed out its SF1.4 mass production to 2029 (vs. 
original 2027). 
On the capacity and yield planning for its advanced nodes, Samsung noted its SF2 yield 
is still at 50-60% levels but confirmed its SF2P+ tooling has been in installation through 
2026 for mass production in 2027 in its Taylor fab. Its capacity expansion in the US 
along with the existing 20k WPM capacity on SF2 in Korea put the company’s capacity 
installed base on the node at 35k WPM by the end of 2027.

### Page 15

Exhibit 28: TSMC's front-end manufacturing transistor density vs. Intel 
TSMC is widening its transistor density advantage against Intel as technology advances

Source: BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH 
Based on the capacity planning and technology roadmap, we believe TSMC retains the 
leadership through its roadmap and yield execution and scale. Specifically, the 
company’s transistor density has been outperforming by ~30% for its N3/N3E vs. Intel 
3/18A. With similar production timing and targeted applications, we estimate the 
transistor density gap could be even widened to ~50%/~60% for TSMC’s upcoming 
A16/A12 nodes when comparing with Intel 18A-P/14A. 
In addition to delivering superior specs on the power, performance and area, TSMC’s 
execution reliability has become self-reinforcing: superior yield and on-time delivery on 
the leading edge technologies win customer trust. The profitable business opportunity 
then translates the cash flow funds to the next leg of capex cycle required for further 
technology and capacity lead. In contrast to TSMC’s 150-200k+ WPM capacity scale on 
every major node at mature yield, Samsung and Intel running their business at 15-20% 
of the company’s scale at 60-70% yield is not sufficient to meet their own demand nor 
the stringent requirement from the external customers especially as the opportunity cost 
gets higher on the advanced manufacturing. 
On the back-end packaging and testing solutions, TSMC provides its customers with 
higher complexity of the system level integration across advanced logic dies, CPO 
components and HBM through its CoWoS and SoIC technologies for the compute 
density and efficiency optimization. To accommodate larger size package, it is rolling out 
5.5-reticle CoWoS in ’26 with yield reaching 98%+ and will push the technology to 
9.5x/14x reticle limit in ’27/’28 
Longer term, the company is developing SoW for up to 40-reticle packaging and CoPoS 
potentially for late ‘28 / early ’29 mass deployment. The company is also upgrading CPO 
with COUPE solutions from PCB and substrate to interposer for 10x power efficiency 
uplift and 20x latency reduction though the contribution would still be limited based on 
our estimate at least through ’27 before accelerating to 1% of its sales in ’28 with more 
scale up switch adoption. 
While CoPoS mass ramp is toward ’29, the CoWoS upgrade at good yield should keep its 
position vs. Intel’s EMIB-T currently at 80-85% pilot yield seeing risk of losing business 
if it fails to deliver 95%+ mass production yield by mid-’27.

### Page 16

SpaceX could be an anchor customer for
TSMC, but not co-owning capacity/tech
On top of the competition risk from the incumbent players, the headline of Elon Musk’s
aggressiveness to build Terafab targeting long term 1 terawatt equivalent chipset
capacity for AI compute deployment in the space term also triggers the investor
concerns on TSMC’s positioning. We believe the risk is overstated as there could be a
delay in the qualification process of Intel’s 18A for external customers and 14A due to IP

and manufacturing flow issues based on the recent supply chain feedback. In addition,
the equipment vendors also noted Terafab is only evaluating on equipment spec and
potential procurement rather than placing firm orders. With extending equipment
delivery lead time, this could put the company in an unfavorable position to receive tools
for the initial production ramp in ’29 it scheduled.
In the mid-term, Terafab’s applications under Texas’s JETI program earlier this year

outlined ~US$119bn of planned investment across 4 phases from ’26-36, with ~60% of
it on tool procurement. The spending in ’26-28 would be highly skewed toward facility
construction before it starts to pull in US$6-8bn equipment per annum from ‘29-36.
We estimate the company will need to spend US$600mn+ for every 1k WPM expansion
on the advanced nodes it targets in the US. Factoring the investment it targets to spend
in the next decade, Terafab could only grow its capacity installed base by ~10k WPM per

annum at maximum to 96k WPM front-end manufacturing capacity installed by ’36, well
below TSMC’s scale, even if it can successfully deliver the technology roadmap through
its own R&D effort and Intel partnership. This would prevent Terafab’s operation from
the economies of scale as the node migration continues, keeping TSMC as the major
foundry partner for Tesla/SpaceX.
Exhibit 29: Wafer supply/demand analysis on Elon Musk's AI deployment target
With Terafab's near term delay and limited long term scale, TSMC will continue to be critical for Musk's ambitious AI compute deployment plan

Wafer supply/demand analysis on Elon Musk's AI deployment target

Source: BofA Global Research estimates
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH
We also analyze the wafer consumption from SpaceX’s annual orbital AI deployment
target in ‘28/’29 of 10GW/100GW and Musk’s long term 1TW target. Assuming the

compute it plans to deploy in ’28-29 is all powered by the most advanced Nvidia’s
Feynman GPUs delivering 6kW per package, this would drive 1.7mn/16.7mn chipset
demand in ’28/’29 respectively which requires 10k/101k monthly advanced wafer
production. From the supply perspective, as Feynman GPU will be built on TSMC’s A16
node, the chipset demand should be fully accommodated. With limited support from
Terafab, SpaceX in this scenario could become one of the largest customers for TSMC

consuming ~20% of its advanced node capacity in ‘29 (vs. ~1% in ’26 and ~2% in ’28).
Longer term, Musk’s ambitious target to deploy 1 terawatt AI compute power annually
would consume 1mn+ wafers per month on the advanced nodes. To have higher level of
the control on capacity sufficiency, Terafab could consider to partner with TSMC though
the likelihood of TSMC licensing technology or operating a fab with Terafab holding a
meaningful share should be low. Specifically, we would note TSMC’s JV fab record is

limited only with WaferTech, SSMC, VIS, JASM, ESMC and AVSM mostly on mature
nodes and it did not co-own a fab with Apple who contributed ~21% of its revenue from
’15-25 mostly on the advanced processes as it keeps full business control and preserves
customer neutrality.

TSMC’s current valuation remains well supported when viewed through both its 
historical context and its relative positioning vs. its customers. In the past decade, 
TSMC’s forward P/E has averaged ~16x with a range of 10-21x through the cycles.

TSMC’s major customers usually have a 22% average premium vs. TSMC in the long 
term though the current level is at 45%, suggesting TSMC’s valuation has been lagging.

### Page 18

Specifically, the valuation deviation for TSMC’s HPC customers vs. the company has 
widened with 100-150% valuation premium vs. TSMC at this stage (vs. historical 
average of 53%). We believe this is driven by a surge in valuation for the direct 
beneficiaries from AI data center deployment, including the companies designing GPUs, 
ASICs and networking chipsets. 
Exhibit 34: TSMC's consensus earnings vs. P/E trend 
TSMC's share price strength only driven by earnings upgrade so far

Source: BofA Global Research, Bloomberg 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH 
It is also noteworthy that, while TSMC’s share performance since 2H23 has been solid, it 
is predominantly driven by the earnings upward revisions rather than multiple expansion. 
The company’s NTM P/E has remained largely stable, only lifted to 18x in the past 2 
years vs. its long-term ~17x levels. The company’s ADR though has been performing 
better as it attracts a broader investor base vs. common share, with its premium still at 
10% (vs. the long term average 8%) and at 20x 2027 consensus P/E. 
With its firmer technology leadership, this should bode well for the company’s earnings 
upgrade cycle and its share should also be re-rated at least 20x NTM P/E (24x P/E for 
ADR factoring the premium), back closer to its customers and the broader ecosystem.

### Page 22

Price objective basis & risk 
Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMWF / TSM) 
Our PO for TSMC is NT$3,260 per share (US$620 per ADR), based on 20x 2027E P/E. 
TSMC's valuation has traded in the range of 10-26x PE from 2017. 20x is in the mid to 
high end of the historical range, above the average P/E of 16x. We think a PE above the 
average is justified given TSMC's enhanced industry position and mid-to-high 50% 5-
year CAGR in AI should underpin a rerating

Downside risks to our price objective are: (1) greater-than-expected slowdown in global 
smartphone/consumer electronics demand, (2) Intel's potential insourcing strategy and 
ambitions in foundry service, (3) execution risks on advanced nodes, and (4) higher than 
expected tariffs.

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
Overall September ODM NB shipments came in 2% below MSe: Quarter-end pull-
ins drove notebook shipments up 32% m/m, although volumes were 2% below MSe,
with weakness mainly coming from Wistron and Compal, and to a lesser extent,
Pegatron, reflecting slower NB shipments from Dell, as Dell accounted for ~60% of

Compal's NB shipment, and ~55% of Wistron's in CY25, on our estimates. Thus total
3Q NB builds came in at 25.2M units (-17% q/q, -26% y/y). This sequential decline
is sub-seasonal vs. the 15Y average seasonality of +5% q/q in 3Q.
Pull-forward demand appears to have come to an end, channel partners and
distributors have stocked up on inventory, and OEMs are slowing their sales into the
channel. We believe this is why our conversations with the ODMs suggest a slightly

weaker 4Q volume outlook than previously expected, despite favorable mix shift.
Thus, we lower our 4Q NB build estimate by 3% to 23.1M (-8% q/q, -29% y/y).
This sequential decline is sub-seasonal (despite an already lowered base in 3Q) vs.
the 15Y average seasonality of flat q/q in 4Q. We forecast October NB builds at
6.8M (-30% m/m, -33% y/y). Our revised forecasts imply CY26 builds of ~107.8M
units (-16% y/y) for the top five Taiwan NB ODMs.

Key Takeaways
Top five ODMs' September 2026 NB shipments were 9.7M units (+32% m/m, -
20% y/y), 2% below our estimate.
Total 3Q26 NB builds came in at 25.2M (-17% q/q, -26% y/y).
We see October 2026 NB builds at 6.8M units (-30% m/m, -33% y/y).
We lower our 4Q26 NB build estimate by 3% to 23.1M units (-8% q/q, -29% y/y).
Our top five ODMs' CY26 NB build estimate is ~107.8M units (-16% y/y).

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

삼성바이오로직스 (207940/KS) 
3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 
2026-10-06

제약/바이오. 이선경 / seonkyoung.lee@sks.co.kr / 3773-9089

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 2,000,000 원(유지) 
현재주가: 1,354,000 원 
상승여력: 47.7%

Signal: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 전망  
Key: 5 공장 매출 본격화, 미국 공장 매출 기여에 따른 성장 기대   
Step: 단기 불확실성보다는 중장기적 성장 방향성에 주목

3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 성장지속, 컨센 부합 전망   
26년 3분기 연결기준 매출액은 1조 3,741억원(+9.3%YoY, +4.0%QoQ), 영업이
익은 6,098억원(-3.7%YoY, +4.0%QoQ, OPM 44.4%)으로 컨센서스 매출액 1
조 3,450억원과 영업이익 6,063억원에 부합하는 실적을 전망한다.  
5월 파업 영향으로 약 20개 배치 생산분(약 1,500억원)에 대한 원가 손실이 인식되
고 3분기 평균 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락했음에도 불구하고, 3분기부터 매출로 
인식되는 5공장 PPQ 배치 및 미국 공장의 매출 기여로 지속적인 성장이 예상된다. 약 
1,500억원 규모의 생산 차질분은 고객사와 일정 조율을 통해 출하될 예정으로 이에 
따른 4분기 매출 성장을 기대해 볼 수 있다.   
신규 수주 회복과 모달리티 확장에 주목 
시장에서 우려했던 신규 수주는 관세 이슈로 고객사의 컨택이 감소하면서 올해 저조했
으나 4 월 Section 232 결과 발표 이후 논의가 회복 단계에 있어 연내 추가 신규 수주 
가능성은 열려 있다.  
항체 시장의 경쟁심화와 차세대 모달리티 확장에 대응하기 위해 결정한 폴리펩타이드
의 인수는 연내 마무리될 예정이다. 폴리펩타이드의 2028 년 목표 EBITDA 마진은 
25%로 동사 대비 낮은 상황이나, 펩타이드 기반 CDMO 시장 성장에 따른 마진 개선
도 가능하다. 현재 임단협, 환율, 미국 공장 및 폴리펩타이드 인수에 따른 회계 영향 등 
불확실성이 큰 상황이나, 수주 회복과 5 공장 매출 인식 본격화, 미국 공장 및 멀티 모
달리티 확장 등 중장기 성장 방향성에 주목할 필요가 있다.

## Document 230: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies  _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.

The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank

world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.
Article completed on 17 September 2026
Stéphane Colliac
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

### Page 3

14 October 2026
3
Summary
Executive
• The Allianz Pulse Survey takes the temperature of European households
each year on the economic, political and societal issues shaping their lives,
with the 2026 edition focusing on artificial intelligence. As AI moves into
everyday life and work, the survey explores how people use the technology,
what they expect from it and how prepared they feel for the changes it will
bring. For its eighth edition, we surveyed a representative sample of 1,000

adults in each of Germany, France, Italy, Spain, Poland and Austria, adding the
UK and US for the first time. This provides a transatlantic perspective on how
people are experiencing and anticipating the AI transition.
• Europe is embracing AI, but adoption is less advanced and less work
related than in the US, with large differences within Europe. Across all eight
countries surveyed, 48% of respondents use AI at least weekly, with the US
and Spain leading at around 55%, while Germany, France and Austria remain

in the low 40s. The generational divide is particularly striking: 77% of Gen Z
use AI weekly, compared with just 23% of Boomers. Moreover, adoption has
yet to translate fully into workplace use: among AI users, 53% use it mainly for
private purposes, 20% mainly for work or study and 27% across both contexts.
Just under half therefore use AI at least partly for work or study, with the
share highest in the US (52%), the UK (51%) and Spain (49%), but only 37%

in Germany. Germany stands out as the country where AI adoption is most
concentrated in private life, while Spain shows that high AI adoption and
workplace use are not uniquely American.
• Europeans see the benefits of AI for themselves, but are considerably
more skeptical about what it means for the economy and jobs, especially
compared with Americans. Net sentiment towards AI is -34% overall, meaning
negative views exceed positive views by 34pp. It deteriorates to -43% for the

economic impact and -67% for jobs. The views about the job impact of AI are
deeply negative, even as positive productivity expectations exceed negative
expectations by 30% across all respondents, while 35% expect little or no
impact. The US provides a clear contrast on employment: its jobs sentiment
stands at -44%, still negative but considerably less so than the survey average.
Within Europe, the picture varies strongly: Austria (-81%) and France (-75%)

are the most pessimistic about the AI impact on jobs, while Spain combines
high AI adoption with less negative attitudes towards AI’s expected job impact
(-66%). Across countries, young, urban and high-income respondents are the
demographic groups most positive about AI. More broadly, the observed AI
skepticism is closely associated with an overall weak confidence in economic
progress: respondents with negative views of AI also tend to hold gloomier

Chiara Rocca
Research Assistant

### Page 4

Allianz Research
4
• As AI adoption spreads, concerns about its impact on jobs are intensifying, even
though most people expect task transformation rather than outright job destruction.
Among countries surveyed in both years, net sentiment on AI’s impact on jobs fell by
around 12pp, from -55% in 2025 to -67% in 2026. The deterioration was particularly
pronounced in Europe: Austria’s jobs balance fell by around 19pps, while Germany and
France also recorded double digit declines. Yet this is not primarily a story of expected

mass unemployment. Only 7% expect their job to disappear entirely, while 21% expect
significant parts to be replaced and 26% expect AI mainly to augment their work.
First-hand experience changes expectations substantially: 41% of workplace AI users
anticipate augmentation, compared with only 11% of non-users, although they are also
more likely to expect task replacement (40% versus 17%). Exposure appears to make
both the risks and opportunities more tangible, particularly the potential for AI to support

rather than replace workers.
• Europe risks entering the AI transition with too little investment in workers, while
training continues to favor those already ahead. Only 17% of all employed respondents
across countries have received AI training at work, compared with 30% in the US. The
lack of training opportunities is a major bottleneck: 44% report that their employer offers
no AI training at all. The European range is wide, with participation falling to just 10%

in Poland, while training is also significantly more common among tertiary educated
workers. This suggests that access to training risks reinforcing existing divides in skills and
opportunity. The transatlantic contrast is particularly concerning: The US combines more
intensive AI use with greater employer-led preparation, while parts of Europe combine
limited workplace adoption with stronger fears of labor-market disruption.
• People fear that AI will widen inequality and want stronger safeguards against

its risks. Across the entire sample, 37% expect AI to increase inequality, 34% think it
will stay the same, compared with only 9% who expect a reduction. Austria records
the strongest expectation of widening inequality, while Poland is the least concerned,
though the balance is positive everywhere. People worry less about what AI might
become than about harms it already causes: misinformation (25%), data privacy (21%)
and job losses (17%) top the list, ahead of loss of control over advanced AI (16%). The net

support for stronger regulation, calculated as the share of respondents in favor minus
the share opposed, increased by 17pp, from +61% in 2025 to +78% in 2026. This does not
signal outright resistance to AI: Spain combines high adoption with strong support for
regulation, showing that enthusiasm for the technology and demand for safeguards can
coexist.
• For Europe, the central challenge is therefore to accelerate productive AI adoption
while ensuring that its benefits are broadly shared. The survey points to five

priorities. First, move AI from private experimentation into productive workplace use
by lowering implementation costs, especially for SMEs. Second, close the training gap
through employer-led, occupation-specific learning. Third, shift labor-market policy
from protecting existing jobs to supporting transitions into new tasks and roles. Fourth,
make sure the productivity dividend is broadly shared through skills, wider household
participation in capital markets and tax systems that adapt to shifts in income from labor

to capital. Fifth, strengthen trust through targeted, proportionate regulation addressing
misinformation, data misuse and labor-market risks without stifling innovation.
Ultimately, success will be measured by AI‘s ability to deliver broadly shared productivity
gains, stronger employability and greater confidence in economic progress.

### Page 5

14 October 2026
AI adoption is unfolding at historically unprecedented
speed, but usage intensity remains uneven. Generative
AI has reached mass adoption faster than earlier
general-purpose technologies such as the personal
computer or the internet, and our survey shows that
more than three-quarters of respondents have already
used AI tools. Yet only 21% use them daily and 22%
have never used them at all (Figure 1). Weekly use
reaches 48% across the sample, led by the US and Spain,

at around 55%, while France, Germany and Austria
remain in the low 40s. The transatlantic difference lies
mainly in intensity rather than reach: the share that has
never used AI is similar in Europe and the US, but daily
use is materially higher in the US, at 30% compared
with around 20% in Europe. Spain shows that high
engagement is not uniquely American.
The largest divides are within societies rather than
across the Atlantic. More than three-quarters, or 77%,

of Gen Z use AI at least weekly, more than three times
the 23% share among Boomers (Figure 2). Millennials
are closest to Gen Z in AI adoption (65% weekly users),
while Gen X (43%) occupy the middle ground. Large gaps
also run through income, education and geography: 59%
of high-income respondents use AI weekly compared
with 40% of low-income respondents, while usage rises
from 40% among the primary-educated to 58% among
those with tertiary education. Urban respondents are

also substantially more engaged than rural ones, at 57%
versus 34%. The gender gap in weekly AI use is evident
but smaller than other gaps: Weekly adoption is higher
among men than women, at 51% versus 45%. Overall,
AI use is already widespread, but the intensity and
economic relevance of that use differs sharply across
demographic groups.
Adoption: Broad but shallow and
still mostly private

AI is still used more in private life than at work. Among
users, 53% employ AI primarily for private purposes,
compared with 20% who use it mainly for work or study
and 27% who use it for both (Figure 3). The private-use
share ranges from 48% in the US to 63% in Germany,
while exclusive work or study use varies only from 14%
in Germany to 23% in France. Combining those who use
AI mainly for work or study with those who use it across
both professional and private contexts, just under half

of users employ AI at least partly for work or study. The
share is highest in the US (52%), the UK (51%) and Spain
(49%), compared with only 37% in Germany. Germany
therefore stands out as the clearest case where AI
adoption remains concentrated in private life. The US
and UK, by contrast, have the highest shares of users
spanning both professional and private contexts, at 33%
and 31%, respectively, suggesting somewhat deeper
integration of AI across different parts of everyday life.

Age matters considerably more than country for how
AI is used. Almost four in five Boomers (78%) use AI
mainly privately, compared with only 8% mainly for work,
although this partly reflects the fact that many in this
age group are already entering or in retirement. Among
Gen Z, the split is almost perfectly balanced: 35% use it
mainly for work or study, 35% mainly privately and 30%
for both. Millennials occupy the middle ground here, with
44% using AI mainly privately, 22% mainly for work or

study and 34% for both. Notably, the gender gap in how
AI is used is surprisingly small: men and women use AI
across private and professional purposes in a similar way,
even though weekly adoption remains somewhat higher
among men than women, at 51% versus 45%.

AI is already widely used, but most people view the
new technology with anxiety rather than enthusiasm
(Figure 4). To capture respondents’ overall attitude
towards AI, we asked them to rate their general
views about the technology on a scale from 0 (very
negative) to 10 (very positive). Using the established
net-promoter-score metric, we classify respondents
into detractors (scores 0-6), passives (scores 7-8) and
promoters (scores 9-10). Across all respondents in the

eight countries surveyed, 52% classify as AI detractors,
compared with just 18% promoters, while 29% fall in
between as passives. In every country surveyed, the
share of detractors far exceeds the share of promoters,
underscoring people’s high levels of uncertainty and
anxiety around AI. Spain stands out as the country with
the lowest share of AI detractors at 40% and the second-
highest share of promotors at 24%. The most positive,
or least negative, AI attitudes are found in the US with

the highest promoter share at 30%. At the other end, the
two countries with the most negative views about AI are
Germany and Austria with only 14% and 11% promoters
but 62% and 60% detractors, respectively.
The divide in AI attitudes is just as striking across
demographic groups, pointing to a broader gap in who
feels comfortable with the AI transition . Promoters are
disproportionately young, urban and high-income. Almost
40% of high-income respondents are promoters, more than

twice the share among low-income respondents (19%),
while only 37% of the high-income group are detractors
compared with 60% of the low-income group. Age shows an
even sharper divide, with two-thirds of Boomers classified
as detractors compared with 39% of Gen Z. Geography
matters too: 66% of rural respondents are detractors,
compared with 45% of urban respondents. Education,
however, tells a more nuanced story. Tertiary-educated
respondents are less likely to reject AI outright than those

with only primary education, but they are not much more
likely to embrace it: promoter shares are 22% and 20%,
respectively. That may reflect the double-edged nature of
AI for knowledge workers. They are better placed to use the
technology, but many also work in occupations where tasks
are especially exposed to the technology.

darker the view

The central finding is a striking gradient in AI

attitudes, which become steadily more negative as
the lens widens from the individual to the economy
and the labor market. Having established who falls
into the promoter, passive and detractor camps, we
use the same classification to compare attitudes across
different dimensions of AI (Figure 5). For each question,
net sentiment is the share of promoters minus the
share of detractors; passives therefore do not affect
the balance. On this measure, sentiment towards AI in

general stands at -34% across the sample, underscoring
the dominant share of detractors. When we widen the
lens to AI’s effect on the economy, net sentiment falls
to -43%. Widen it once more to AI’s effect on jobs and it
drops to a stark -67%, the most negative reading in the
entire survey. The latter number means that the 75%
of respondents with negative assessments outnumber
the 8% of respondents with positive ones by 67pp. This
gradient shows that people are relaxed about the tool in

their own hands but fearful of it in the labor market. No
single country or demographic group escapes this pattern.
Across countries, the same downward gradient holds,
but the intensity of AI skepticism varies sharply: the US
is the most positive, Austria and France are the most
negative, while Spain combines high overall enthusiasm
with pronounced concern about jobs . Respondents
from the US have consistently the mildest view on AI
across general attitude, impact on the economy and

jobs. In particular, the US reading of a net AI sentiment
on jobs of -44%, stemming from 20% promoters and 64%
detractors is the highest in the sample, although it is
still deeply negative. On the other end of the spectrum,
Austria is the most skeptical throughout, with net
sentiment on AI’s impact on jobs falling to -81%, followed
closely by France at -75%. Spain provides perhaps

the most revealing contrast: it is the most enthusiastic

As AI adoption accelerates, public sentiment is moving in
the opposite direction: attitudes towards the technology
have deteriorated across the board over the past year.
Comparing responses with 2025, sentiment towards
AI in general has edged lower, views of its economic
impact have become more negative and concerns about

jobs have intensified most sharply. The net balance on
employment plunged by 12 pp, from -55% to -67%, with
Austria recording a particularly steep decline of around
19pp and Germany and France also seeing double-digit
drops. The bottom line: the more widespread AI becomes,
the more uneasy people feel about its consequences. And
these concerns may have deepened further, as the survey
predates the latest public debate around advanced AI
and its safety risks.

The one bright spot is the personal frame, where more
people expect AI to help rather than to harm them, and
productivity and trust move together (Figure 6). More
respondents expect AI to raise their own productivity
rather than to lower it, for a net of +30%. A slim majority
trust the information AI provides, for a net of +18%. Trust
and expected productivity move closely together across
countries and demographic groups. France sits at the
skeptical end, with net distrust of AI information at -5%,

alongside Austria at close to zero and Boomers at -4%.
At the other end are Spain (+43%), the US (+36%) and
younger generations.

Every year, the Allianz Pulse tracks how households view the economy and their own prospects. In 2026, economic
confidence fell back toward pandemic-era lows, raising a new question: Could growing fears about AI be adding
to pessimism about both the near-term economic outlook and future generations’ prospects for prosperity? As
AI becomes part of everyday life and work, its potential to transform jobs, incomes and living standards adds a new

dimension to how people assess their economic future.
Economic sentiment has deteriorated sharply across most countries. Since 2019, the Allianz Pulse has tracked
assessments of current economic conditions and future prospects. Our economic sentiment indicator averages the net
balances of positive minus negative responses to both questions. In 2026, it stands at -67% in France, -56% in Germany,
-50% in Italy and -46% in Austria (Figure 7). Germany recorded the sharpest reversal, falling from -17% in 2025 to -56%,

below even its reading during the Covid pandemic. Poland remains the only positive outlier at +9%, while the UK (-38%)
reflects the subdued mood across Western Europe. The US, included for the first time, is close to neutral at -2%.

Economic pessimism and AI skepticism are closely intertwined.  Among respondents who assess the economy as “bad”,

61% have negative views about AI and only 14% are positive about it. Among those who see the economy as “good”, the
picture is almost reversed: Just 24% are AI detractors, while 49% are promoters (Figure 8). The relationship is gradual:
Moving from a “fairly bad” to a “fairly good” assessment reduces the detractor share from 56% to 41% and raises the
promoter share from 14% to 24%. However, the direction of the relationship remains unclear. Economic pessimism may

fuel AI skepticism, concerns about AI may weigh on confidence, or both may reflect broader anxieties about the future.
The relationship holds across countries, but its strength varies considerably.  It is particularly pronounced in the US,
where the share of AI detractors falls from 62% among respondents who rate the economy as bad to just 12% among
those who rate it as good, while the promoter share rises from 19% to 68%. The UK and Germany show similarly steep

### Page 13

14 October 2026
13
Beyond the immediate economic outlook, confidence in generational progress is also fading. Only 18% of
respondents expect the next generation to be better off than today, while 50% expect it to be worse off, resulting in a
net balance of -32%. Pessimism increases with age: The balance stands at -14% among Gen Z but falls to -43% among
Boomers. The generation whose future is at stake is thus substantially more optimistic than older cohorts. The US stands

out: Expectations for the next generation are close to neutral overall, and young Americans are the only group in the
survey that expects future generations to be better off.
Doubts about generational progress go hand in hand with more negative views of AI. Only 18% of respondents
expect the next generation to be better off than today, while half expect it to be worse off. Among respondents who
expect the next generation to be worse off, 57% are AI detractors and only 13% promoters. Among those who expect

an improvement, detractors and promoters are almost evenly matched, at 36% and 33%, respectively (Figure 9). This
translates into a roughly 40pp difference in net AI sentiment, from -44% among generational pessimists to close to
neutral (-4%) among optimists. The relationship holds across countries, although national differences remain significant.
Among those expecting generational progress, net AI sentiment is positive in the US (+34%), the UK (+23%) and Spain

(+3%), but remains negative in Germany, France and Austria.
The US offers a glimpse of how attitudes might evolve once the economic benefits of AI become more tangible
AI-related investment, including information-processing equipment, software, R&D and data centers, accounted for
an estimated 39% of US real GDP growth in the first three quarters of 2025¹. At the same time, rising equity valuations
have boosted household wealth, with AI enthusiasm playing an important role. This wealth channel is particularly

also as an investment and wealth boom². This may help explain why both economic sentiment and AI attitudes are
less negative in the US, although the benefits remain unevenly distributed. The central challenge is to ensure that AI

strengthens confidence in economic progress rather than becoming another source of insecurity. This will depend not
only on delivering productivity gains but also on making their benefits visible, tangible and broadly shared.

15
People are deeply pessimistic about AI’s impact on
jobs in general, yet relatively few expect their own
job to disappear. The bigger concern is how tasks will
change and who will be able to adapt (Figure 10). Net
sentiment towards AI’s impact on jobs stands at -67%,
the most negative reading on AI attitudes in the survey.
However, only 7% of respondents expect AI to replace
their own job entirely within the next five years. A further

21% expect significant parts of their work to be replaced,
while 26% expect AI mainly to assist and augment what
they do. Another 25% foresee little or no impact, while
20% do not know. The apparent paradox between very
negative views of AI’s overall impact on employment
and relatively limited fears of personal job loss can
therefore be reconciled: people expect AI to reshape
tasks and job content even if they do not expect mass
unemployment (see our forecast on the macroeconomic

and labor market impact of AI in Box 2).
Across countries, the main difference is not whether
people expect change, but whether they see AI primarily
as a substitute, a complement or an unknown. The US
records the highest expectation of outright displacement:
12% expect their job to disappear entirely, almost twice
the European average, and another 21% expect significant
parts to be replaced. Spain also anticipates substantial
change, with 25% expecting significant task replacement,

but this is matched by the highest share expecting AI to
assist their work (31%, alongside Austria) and the lowest
level of uncertainty in the sample at just 13%. Austria
similarly leans towards augmentation, with 31% expecting
AI to assist and support them. France and Germany, by
contrast, stand out less for replacement fears than for
uncertainty: around one in four respondents do not know
how AI will affect their job. These differences suggest
that labor-market anxiety has different forms across

countries: substitution fears are more pronounced in the
US, expectations of task reconfiguration are strongest in
Spain, while parts of Europe remain unsure about what the
transition will mean in practice.

disruption rather than job
destruction
